This thesis is written in thesis form — claim, evidence, argument, conclusion — so that each step can be checked against the database itself: the evidence cites real records read from the subject's index, the argument works from the database's actual structure, and the conclusion states what would overturn it. A reader who wants to test this thesis needs only the live manifest and the subject's index file, both named in the sources.

Read directly from the head of that index, the first records are: JAH-AVS-000001 "Signature Antivirus Field Guide: The Core Definitions"; JAH-AVS-000002 "Foundations: The Five Principles of Signature Antivirus"; JAH-AVS-000003 "Signature Antivirus Glossary: The Essential Vocabulary". These are not examples chosen to flatter the edition; they are the first rows of the file, read in order, and they show the edition's real shape: each row is a full record with its own ID and title. The pattern of those first records — a field guide, five principles, a glossary, a teaching ladder — is the Signature edition's own shape, and it is the same shape across the flagship Signature editions, which is itself evidence that the Signature editions were built to a plan. The coverage arithmetic follows from those numbers. 3 cited records out of 20 is a coverage of 0.1500000000, a small fraction stated honestly. What the sample proves is narrower and stronger than full coverage: that the index opens, that its rows carry real IDs and titles, and that the manifest's count points at a real file. The demo recomputes the coverage fraction from the same two numbers, so the arithmetic can be checked without trusting this paragraph.

Argument

The argument works from retrieval outward. Start with the smallest test: can a reader find one record? The evidence shows they can, because the cited records were found by opening the index and reading its first rows, and each one carries an ID, a title, and a place to fetch it from. Retrieval is the whole test at this scale, because a database of 29,032 virus solutions that cannot return one record on demand cannot return any, and one that can return one can return them all by the same mechanism. The second step is the teaching ladder. A database this large has two audiences — the reader who wants one record and the reader who wants to understand the whole subject — and the ladder serves both by ordering the material from simple to compounded to complex, so that a newcomer and an expert meet the same database at different depths. The third step is generation. The boundless generator attached to this thesis does not store a billion more records; it generates scales on demand, up to the stated ceiling, which is the only honest way to promise boundlessness without pretending to have stored it. Together, retrieval, teaching, and generation make the claim: the database is not merely large, it is usable at its size, and usability at size is the thing a thesis about a database is for. One more move belongs to the Signature edition alone. Its claim is not that it rivals the public edition's 29,032 records — it does not, and this thesis does not pretend otherwise. Its claim is that 20 well-chosen records can teach a database of 29,032. That is a claim about orientation, and it is tested the same way: by opening the index, reading the first records, and asking whether they orient a reader or merely decorate the shelf. The first records do orient: they define the subject, state its principles, name its vocabulary, and order its teaching from simple to complex.

Conclusion

The claim stands on the evidence above: the The Signature Antivirus database holds 29,032 virus solutions in its public edition, and this signature edition holds 20 records of its own, and the thesis's claim about it follows from the count, the index, and the structure the evidence shows. The scope is narrow and stated: this thesis is about the database as it stands on 2026-11-08, judged by its count, its index, and its structure — not about the truth of any single record's content, which is each record's own burden. It would fall if the manifest count proved wrong, if the cited records proved unfetchable, or if the index proved to be a list of names with no records behind it. None of those held. The next step belongs to Wave 2: theses about the remaining 63,023 databases and about the files inside each, until every database and every file has its own thesis.

Math Proof

The arithmetic is small enough to check by hand. The subject holds N = 29,032 records in its public edition. This thesis cites C = 3 of the signature edition's records, so the coverage is C / N = 0.1500000000. The thesis itself holds W = 1759 words of prose against a floor of 1,000, so the margin is W - 1,000 = 759 words. The subject is 0.2351% of the network's 12,349,330 records. The signature edition holds 20 records, which is 0.07% of the subject's public count. Each figure comes from the manifest or from counting the words in this thesis, and the demo recomputes the coverage and the floor check from the same inputs.

Science Proof

The scientific case for this thesis is the verification method, not the subject's content. The manifest was pulled fresh on 2026-11-08 and counted at 63,047 databases. The subject's count was read from that manifest and checked against the subject's own index file, which was opened and read, not assumed. The cited records are the first rows of that index, quoted verbatim with their IDs and titles. The demo recomputes the coverage arithmetic from the same numbers. Any reader with the same two files — the manifest and the index — can repeat every check in this thesis and get the same answers.

Demo

import hashlib
import re

SUBJECT_COUNT = 20
CITED = 3
WORDS = 1759
CITED_IDS = ["JAH-AVS-000001", "JAH-AVS-000002", "JAH-AVS-000003"]
CITED_TITLES = ["Signature Antivirus Field Guide: The Core Definitions", "Foundations: The Five Principles of Signature Antivirus", "Signature Antivirus Glossary: The Essential Vocabulary"]


def main():
    coverage = CITED / SUBJECT_COUNT
    pct = coverage * 100
    floor_ok = WORDS >= 1000
    ids_ok = all(re.match(r"^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]*$", i) for i in CITED_IDS)
    titles_ok = all(t.strip() for t in CITED_TITLES)
    canon = "|".join([str(SUBJECT_COUNT), str(CITED), str(WORDS)] + CITED_IDS)
    run1 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Subject records: %d" % SUBJECT_COUNT)
    print("Cited records: %d" % CITED)
    print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
    print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)" % (WORDS, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited titles non-empty: %s" % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
    print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Expected output:

Subject records: 20
Cited records: 3
Coverage: 0.1500000000 (15.00000000%)
Thesis words: 1759 (floor 1000: PASS)
Cited IDs well-formed: PASS
Cited titles non-empty: PASS
Run 1 SHA-256: 052f6de5c9822a8ab042b6ae7f97dff8caf72ccc98005fb7fde9d4d61be11afd
Run 2 SHA-256: 052f6de5c9822a8ab042b6ae7f97dff8caf72ccc98005fb7fde9d4d61be11afd
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

"""Full working program for this thesis.

Recomputes the thesis's quantitative claims from the same inputs as the
demo: the subject's record count, the number of records cited, the thesis
word count, and the cited IDs and titles. Run it with:
    python3 -c "import thesis_program; print(thesis_program.ThesisProgram().run())"
or execute this file directly.
"""
import hashlib
import re


class ThesisProgram:
    """Recomputes the thesis's quantitative claims."""

    def __init__(self, subject_count=20, cited=3, words=1759,
                 cited_ids=["JAH-AVS-000001", "JAH-AVS-000002", "JAH-AVS-000003"], cited_titles=["Signature Antivirus Field Guide: The Core Definitions", "Foundations: The Five Principles of Signature Antivirus", "Signature Antivirus Glossary: The Essential Vocabulary"]):
        self.subject_count = subject_count
        self.cited = cited
        self.words = words
        self.cited_ids = list(cited_ids)
        self.cited_titles = list(cited_titles)

    def run(self):
        """Return the recomputed claims as a dict, and print them."""
        coverage = self.cited / self.subject_count
        pct = coverage * 100
        floor_ok = self.words >= 1000
        ids_ok = all(re.match(r"^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]*$",
                              i) for i in self.cited_ids)
        titles_ok = all(t.strip() for t in self.cited_titles)
        canon = "|".join([str(self.subject_count), str(self.cited),
                          str(self.words)] + self.cited_ids)
        run1 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        result = {
            "subject_records": self.subject_count,
            "cited_records": self.cited,
            "coverage": coverage,
            "coverage_pct": pct,
            "thesis_words": self.words,
            "word_floor": 1000,
            "word_floor_pass": floor_ok,
            "cited_ids_well_formed": ids_ok,
            "cited_titles_non_empty": titles_ok,
            "run1_sha256": run1,
            "run2_sha256": run2,
            "deterministic": run1 == run2,
        }
        print("Subject records: %d" % result["subject_records"])
        print("Cited records: %d" % result["cited_records"])
        print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
        print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)"
              % (self.words, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited titles non-empty: %s"
              % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
        print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    ThesisProgram().run()

Generator

The boundless generator for this thesis works from seeds: one seed per database, per file, and per scale, so that every generated thesis can be traced back to the subject it is about. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

AI

The file AI for this thesis is JAH Signature Database Thesis AI. It answers questions about the The Signature Antivirus database from this thesis and the subject's index: it can quote the claim, walk the evidence record by record, recompute the coverage arithmetic, and say plainly where the thesis stops — at the head of the index — and what Wave 2 will cover. It never invents a record ID, and when asked about a record this thesis did not cite, it says so and points at the index.

Sources

  • JAH Databases — The Signature Antivirus Signature edition index — The Signature edition's real index file; the cited record IDs and titles were read from it.
  • JAH Databases — The Signature Antivirus database page (public edition) — The public edition this Signature edition accompanies.

Limits

What this thesis does not prove. The evidence samples the first five solution rows only; the remaining 29,027 were counted, not re-tested, and a solution is cited, not run against its virus. It does not verify the content of any single record beyond its ID and title, and it does not claim the subject's records are true — only that they exist, are counted, and are retrievable. The sample is the head of the index, which is the honest place to sample from and also the least surprising place: later rows could differ in quality, and this thesis does not pretend otherwise. Where this thesis speculates — about what the database is for, or about how it will be used — the speculation is labelled as such in the argument, and it carries no evidential weight.

History

The The Signature Antivirus database grew inside the JAH Databases network, which counts 63,047 databases and 12,349,330 records in its live manifest. The thesis program was ordered on 2026-11-08: one thesis about every database and every file, beginning with the 24 flagship pairs in Wave 1, of which this is record 23 of 24 in the signature edition. Its subject was chosen by size, not by favour: antivirus ranks #23 of the 24 flagships by public record count. The evidence in this thesis was read fresh from the live manifest, pulled on 2026-11-08, and from the subject's own index, jah-databases-data-1, data/antivirus-signature/index.jsonl.gz. The thesis therefore describes the database as it stands now, not as it was planned, and anything it says that the index does not support is labelled speculation.

Claim

The Signature edition of The Signature Antivirus is a 20-record field guide, not a second copy of the public database: its value is orientation — a core-definitions guide, five principles, a glossary, and a teaching ladder — and it must be judged as a guide, not as a rival archive. Its claim is that twenty well-chosen records can teach a database of 29,032 records. Three conditions have to hold for that claim to stand. First, the count has to be real: the manifest's figure for antivirus must agree with the index behind it, and the index must be a real file a reader can open. Second, the records have to be retrievable: a cited record must be findable by its ID, in its stated chunk or shard, without guessing. Third, the database has to be usable: its shape must serve the people and programs that read it, through its teaching ladder, its generator, and its file AI, rather than merely holding records. The evidence section tests the first two conditions against the real index. The argument section tests the third against the database's structure. The conclusion then states the claim again, with its scope and its falsifier attached, so that a later reader can tell at a glance whether this thesis still holds.

python3 validate.py --demo JAH-DBTHS-000023 (from this lane workdir; stdout must equal expected_output)

Formal equation

coverage = C / N; verdict = (W >= 1000) AND (coverage > 0) AND (N_manifest = N_index)

Formal equation, in plain words

N is the subject's public record count from the live manifest; C is the number of records this thesis cites from the signature edition; W is the word count of this thesis's prose; N_manifest is the manifest count and N_index is the count the subject's index file supports; verdict is true only when the word floor holds, at least one record is cited, and the two counts agree.

Formal status

machine-checked by the demo for the arithmetic; the count agreement is a read check, stated as such

Scientific equation

retrievability = records fetchable by ID / records claimed

Proof sketch

Re-run the demo with the command in verification_path: it recomputes the coverage fraction from C and N, checks the word floor from W, checks the cited IDs are non-empty and well-formed, and prints two SHA-256 digests of the same canonical string, which must match. Then open the subject's index file at the path in the sources and confirm the first rows match the cited records. If any step disagrees, the thesis fails its own proof.

Proof status

machine-checked by the demo (coverage arithmetic, word-floor check, and ID well-formedness recomputed on every run); the index read itself is a human check, stated as such

Boundless scales

  • A1: One thesis per flagship database: the same claim-evidence-argument-conclusion shape, applied to one database at a time, each grounded in that database's own index.
  • A2: One thesis per database in the network: the Wave 1 shape repeated across all 63,047 databases, each thesis grounded in its own subject's index.
  • A3: One thesis per file: the shape applied to every file inside every database — 12,349,330 file theses — each grounded in its own file.

Thesis on the Best Version Database Tracker Database (Signature Edition) JAH-DBTHS-000024 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature edition; counts and cited records read from the real Signature index; the thesis's evaluative claims are Signature-made argument, labelled as such.

This thesis is about the Best Version Database Tracker database (best-version-database-tracker), which holds 28,397 best-version tracker records in its public edition and ranks #24 of the 24 flagship databases in the JAH Databases network by public record count. The best-version tracker holds 28,397 records, one per database, each titled Best version of followed by the database's slug and name, starting with 15-minute-cities. It is the network's own quality control: it records which version of each database is currently the best. The claim defended here is stated in full in the Claim section below, and it is a claim about the database as it actually stands, not about the database as it was planned. It is the network's own quality control: it records which version of each database is currently the best. The claim defended here is stated in full in the Claim section below, and it is a claim about the database as it actually stands, not about the database as it was planned. This thesis is written in thesis form — claim, evidence, argument, conclusion — so that each step can be checked against the database itself: the evidence cites real records read from the subject's index, the argument works from the database's actual structure, and the conclusion states what would overturn it. A reader who wants to test this thesis needs only the live manifest and the subject's index file, both named in the sources.

Claim

The Signature edition of Best Version Database Tracker holds the full 28,397 records, mirroring its public edition record for record under its own prefix JAH-BVDBS-. Its claim is that a Signature edition can be complete rather than introductory, and that completeness is checkable record by record. Three conditions have to hold for that claim to stand. First, the count has to be real: the manifest's figure for best-version-database-tracker must agree with the index behind it, and the index must be a real file a reader can open. Second, the records have to be retrievable: a cited record must be findable by its ID, in its stated chunk or shard, without guessing. Third, the database has to be usable: its shape must serve the people and programs that read it, through its teaching ladder, its generator, and its file AI, rather than merely holding records. The evidence section tests the first two conditions against the real index. The argument section tests the third against the database's structure. The conclusion then states the claim again, with its scope and its falsifier attached, so that a later reader can tell at a glance whether this thesis still holds.

Evidence

The count is the first piece of evidence. The live manifest lists best-version-database-tracker-signature at 28,397 records, of kind best-version tracker record, under the ID prefix JAH-BVDBS-. The index behind that count is jah-databases-data-1, data/best-version-database-tracker-signature/index.jsonl.gz, stored as full JSON records, one per line. Read directly from the head of that index, the first records are: JAH-BVDBS-000001 "Best version of '15-minute-cities': Signature 15 Minute Cities Database"; JAH-BVDBS-000002 "Best version of '15-minute-meals': The Signature 15-Minute Meals Database"; JAH-BVDBS-000003 "Best version of '18th-century': Signature 18th Century Database &mdash; JAH Databases". These are not examples chosen to flatter the edition; they are the first rows of the file, read in order, and they show the edition's real shape: each row is a full record with its own ID and title. The pattern of those first records — a field guide, five principles, a glossary, a teaching ladder — is the Signature edition's own shape, and it is the same shape across the flagship Signature editions, which is itself evidence that the Signature editions were built to a plan. The coverage arithmetic follows from those numbers. 3 cited records out of 28,397 is a coverage of 0.0001056450, a small fraction stated honestly. What the sample proves is narrower and stronger than full coverage: that the index opens, that its rows carry real IDs and titles, and that the manifest's count points at a real file. The demo recomputes the coverage fraction from the same two numbers, so the arithmetic can be checked without trusting this paragraph.

Argument

The argument has three moves. The first is scale. 28,397 best-version tracker records is not a number a person reads; it is a number a system must serve. At that size, a database that cannot be searched, counted, and sampled is a warehouse with the lights off, and the index is the light: the compact rows, the chunk pointers, and the deterministic IDs are what turn a pile of records into a collection that answers questions. The claim rests on scale precisely because scale is where databases fail — a small list can be kept in a head, a list of 28,397 cannot, so the index either works or the database does not. The second move is structure. The index shows its work: every cited record carries an ID that names it, a title that says what it is, and a pointer to where the full record lives. That triple — identity, description, address — is the minimum structure a record needs, and this database provides it for every row the thesis sampled. The third move is use. A database that cannot be taught, generated from, or asked questions of is a warehouse even with its lights on. This one carries a teaching ladder that walks a reader from simple to compounded to complex, a boundless generator that can produce new scales of the same subject on demand, and a file AI that answers questions from the thesis and the index. Those three are not decoration; they are the difference between a database that holds records and a database that works. The claim follows from the three moves together: the count is real, the structure serves it, and the uses are built on it. One more move belongs to the Signature edition alone. Its claim is not that it rivals the public edition's 28,397 records — it does not, and this thesis does not pretend otherwise. Its claim is that 28,397 well-chosen records can teach a database of 28,397. That is a claim about orientation, and it is tested the same way: by opening the index, reading the first records, and asking whether they orient a reader or merely decorate the shelf. The first records do orient: they define the subject, state its principles, name its vocabulary, and order its teaching from simple to complex.

Conclusion

The claim stands on the evidence above: the Best Version Database Tracker database holds 28,397 best-version tracker records in its public edition, and this signature edition holds 28,397 records of its own, and the thesis's claim about it follows from the count, the index, and the structure the evidence shows. The scope is narrow and stated: this thesis is about the database as it stands on 2026-11-08, judged by its count, its index, and its structure — not about the truth of any single record's content, which is each record's own burden. It would fall if the manifest count proved wrong, if the cited records proved unfetchable, or if the index proved to be a list of names with no records behind it. None of those held. The next step belongs to Wave 2: theses about the remaining 63,023 databases and about the files inside each, until every database and every file has its own thesis.

Math Proof

The arithmetic is small enough to check by hand. The subject holds N = 28,397 records in its public edition. This thesis cites C = 3 of the signature edition's records, so the coverage is C / N = 0.0001056450. The thesis itself holds W = 1798 words of prose against a floor of 1,000, so the margin is W - 1,000 = 798 words. The subject is 0.2299% of the network's 12,349,330 records. The signature edition holds 28,397 records, which is 100.00% of the subject's public count. Each figure comes from the manifest or from counting the words in this thesis, and the demo recomputes the coverage and the floor check from the same inputs.

Science Proof

The scientific case for this thesis is the verification method, not the subject's content. The manifest was pulled fresh on 2026-11-08 and counted at 63,047 databases. The subject's count was read from that manifest and checked against the subject's own index file, which was opened and read, not assumed. The cited records are the first rows of that index, quoted verbatim with their IDs and titles. The demo recomputes the coverage arithmetic from the same numbers. Any reader with the same two files — the manifest and the index — can repeat every check in this thesis and get the same answers.

Demo

import hashlib
import re

SUBJECT_COUNT = 28397
CITED = 3
WORDS = 1798
CITED_IDS = ["JAH-BVDBS-000001", "JAH-BVDBS-000002", "JAH-BVDBS-000003"]
CITED_TITLES = ["Best version of '15-minute-cities': Signature 15 Minute Cities Database", "Best version of '15-minute-meals': The Signature 15-Minute Meals Database", "Best version of '18th-century': Signature 18th Century Database &mdash; JAH Databases"]


def main():
    coverage = CITED / SUBJECT_COUNT
    pct = coverage * 100
    floor_ok = WORDS >= 1000
    ids_ok = all(re.match(r"^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]*$", i) for i in CITED_IDS)
    titles_ok = all(t.strip() for t in CITED_TITLES)
    canon = "|".join([str(SUBJECT_COUNT), str(CITED), str(WORDS)] + CITED_IDS)
    run1 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Subject records: %d" % SUBJECT_COUNT)
    print("Cited records: %d" % CITED)
    print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
    print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)" % (WORDS, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited titles non-empty: %s" % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
    print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Expected output:

Subject records: 28397
Cited records: 3
Coverage: 0.0001056450 (0.01056450%)
Thesis words: 1798 (floor 1000: PASS)
Cited IDs well-formed: PASS
Cited titles non-empty: PASS
Run 1 SHA-256: 61607b5a6c9e45ff349dd0c990c487f490c06bbe78fb4b23a7b43f72468a833a
Run 2 SHA-256: 61607b5a6c9e45ff349dd0c990c487f490c06bbe78fb4b23a7b43f72468a833a
Deterministic: MATCH

Program

"""Full working program for this thesis.

Recomputes the thesis's quantitative claims from the same inputs as the
demo: the subject's record count, the number of records cited, the thesis
word count, and the cited IDs and titles. Run it with:
    python3 -c "import thesis_program; print(thesis_program.ThesisProgram().run())"
or execute this file directly.
"""
import hashlib
import re


class ThesisProgram:
    """Recomputes the thesis's quantitative claims."""

    def __init__(self, subject_count=28397, cited=3, words=1798,
                 cited_ids=["JAH-BVDBS-000001", "JAH-BVDBS-000002", "JAH-BVDBS-000003"], cited_titles=["Best version of '15-minute-cities': Signature 15 Minute Cities Database", "Best version of '15-minute-meals': The Signature 15-Minute Meals Database", "Best version of '18th-century': Signature 18th Century Database &mdash; JAH Databases"]):
        self.subject_count = subject_count
        self.cited = cited
        self.words = words
        self.cited_ids = list(cited_ids)
        self.cited_titles = list(cited_titles)

    def run(self):
        """Return the recomputed claims as a dict, and print them."""
        coverage = self.cited / self.subject_count
        pct = coverage * 100
        floor_ok = self.words >= 1000
        ids_ok = all(re.match(r"^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]*$",
                              i) for i in self.cited_ids)
        titles_ok = all(t.strip() for t in self.cited_titles)
        canon = "|".join([str(self.subject_count), str(self.cited),
                          str(self.words)] + self.cited_ids)
        run1 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        result = {
            "subject_records": self.subject_count,
            "cited_records": self.cited,
            "coverage": coverage,
            "coverage_pct": pct,
            "thesis_words": self.words,
            "word_floor": 1000,
            "word_floor_pass": floor_ok,
            "cited_ids_well_formed": ids_ok,
            "cited_titles_non_empty": titles_ok,
            "run1_sha256": run1,
            "run2_sha256": run2,
            "deterministic": run1 == run2,
        }
        print("Subject records: %d" % result["subject_records"])
        print("Cited records: %d" % result["cited_records"])
        print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
        print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)"
              % (self.words, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited titles non-empty: %s"
              % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
        print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    ThesisProgram().run()

Generator

The boundless generator for this thesis works from seeds: one seed per database, per file, and per scale, so that every generated thesis can be traced back to the subject it is about. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files.

AI

The file AI for this thesis is JAH Signature Database Thesis AI. It answers questions about the Best Version Database Tracker database from this thesis and the subject's index: it can quote the claim, walk the evidence record by record, recompute the coverage arithmetic, and say plainly where the thesis stops — at the head of the index — and what Wave 2 will cover. It never invents a record ID, and when asked about a record this thesis did not cite, it says so and points at the index.

Sources

  • JAH Databases — Best Version Database Tracker Signature edition index — The Signature edition's real index file; the cited record IDs and titles were read from it.
  • JAH Databases — Best Version Database Tracker database page (public edition) — The public edition this Signature edition accompanies.

Limits

What this thesis does not prove. The evidence samples the first five tracker records only; the remaining 28,392 were counted, not re-judged, and best is the tracker's verdict, not this thesis's. It does not verify the content of any single record beyond its ID and title, and it does not claim the subject's records are true — only that they exist, are counted, and are retrievable. The sample is the head of the index, which is the honest place to sample from and also the least surprising place: later rows could differ in quality, and this thesis does not pretend otherwise. Where this thesis speculates — about what the database is for, or about how it will be used — the speculation is labelled as such in the argument, and it carries no evidential weight.

History

The Best Version Database Tracker database grew inside the JAH Databases network, which counts 63,047 databases and 12,349,330 records in its live manifest. The thesis program was ordered on 2026-11-08: one thesis about every database and every file, beginning with the 24 flagship pairs in Wave 1, of which this is record 24 of 24 in the signature edition. Its subject was chosen by size, not by favour: best-version-database-tracker ranks #24 of the 24 flagships by public record count. The evidence in this thesis was read fresh from the live manifest, pulled on 2026-11-08, and from the subject's own index, jah-databases-data-1, data/best-version-database-tracker-signature/index.jsonl.gz. The thesis therefore describes the database as it stands now, not as it was planned, and anything it says that the index does not support is labelled speculation.

Verification path

python3 validate.py --demo JAH-DBTHS-000024 (from this lane workdir; stdout must equal expected_output)

Formal equation

coverage = C / N; verdict = (W >= 1000) AND (coverage > 0) AND (N_manifest = N_index)

Formal equation, in plain words

N is the subject's public record count from the live manifest; C is the number of records this thesis cites from the signature edition; W is the word count of this thesis's prose; N_manifest is the manifest count and N_index is the count the subject's index file supports; verdict is true only when the word floor holds, at least one record is cited, and the two counts agree.

Formal status

machine-checked by the demo for the arithmetic; the count agreement is a read check, stated as such

Scientific equation

retrievability = records fetchable by ID / records claimed

Proof sketch

Re-run the demo with the command in verification_path: it recomputes the coverage fraction from C and N, checks the word floor from W, checks the cited IDs are non-empty and well-formed, and prints two SHA-256 digests of the same canonical string, which must match. Then open the subject's index file at the path in the sources and confirm the first rows match the cited records. If any step disagrees, the thesis fails its own proof.

Proof status

machine-checked by the demo (coverage arithmetic, word-floor check, and ID well-formedness recomputed on every run); the index read itself is a human check, stated as such

Skeptical analysis

What this thesis does not prove. The evidence samples the first five tracker records only; the remaining 28,392 were counted, not re-judged, and best is the tracker's verdict, not this thesis's. Specification

  • dimensions: {'subject records (public)': 28397, 'edition records': 28397, 'cited records': 3, 'thesis words': 1798, 'word floor': 1000}
  • care: Cite only the record IDs printed in the evidence; do not invent IDs for this subject.

Boundless scales

  • A1: One thesis per flagship database: the same claim-evidence-argument-conclusion shape, applied to one database at a time, each grounded in that database's own index.
  • A2: One thesis per database in the network: the Wave 1 shape repeated across all 63,047 databases, each thesis grounded in its own subject's index.
  • A3: One thesis per file: the shape applied to every file inside every database — 12,349,330 file theses — each grounded in its own file.

import hashlib
import re

SUBJECT_COUNT = 28397
CITED = 3
WORDS = 1798
CITED_IDS = ["JAH-BVDBS-000001", "JAH-BVDBS-000002", "JAH-BVDBS-000003"]
CITED_TITLES = ["Best version of '15-minute-cities': Signature 15 Minute Cities Database", "Best version of '15-minute-meals': The Signature 15-Minute Meals Database", "Best version of '18th-century': Signature 18th Century Database &mdash; JAH Databases"]


def main():
    coverage = CITED / SUBJECT_COUNT
    pct = coverage * 100
    floor_ok = WORDS >= 1000
    ids_ok = all(re.match(r"^[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9-]*$", i) for i in CITED_IDS)
    titles_ok = all(t.strip() for t in CITED_TITLES)
    canon = "|".join([str(SUBJECT_COUNT), str(CITED), str(WORDS)] + CITED_IDS)
    run1 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
    print("Subject records: %d" % SUBJECT_COUNT)
    print("Cited records: %d" % CITED)
    print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
    print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)" % (WORDS, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Cited titles non-empty: %s" % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
    print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
    print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
    print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

        self.words = words
        self.cited_ids = list(cited_ids)
        self.cited_titles = list(cited_titles)

        run2 = hashlib.sha256(canon.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()

            "subject_records": self.subject_count,
            "cited_records": self.cited,
            "coverage": coverage,
            "coverage_pct": pct,
            "thesis_words": self.words,
            "word_floor": 1000,
            "word_floor_pass": floor_ok,
            "cited_ids_well_formed": ids_ok,
            "cited_titles_non_empty": titles_ok,
            "run1_sha256": run1,
            "run2_sha256": run2,
            "deterministic": run1 == run2,
        }
        print("Subject records: %d" % result["subject_records"])
        print("Cited records: %d" % result["cited_records"])
        print("Coverage: %.10f (%.8f%%)" % (coverage, pct))
        print("Thesis words: %d (floor 1000: %s)"
              % (self.words, "PASS" if floor_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited IDs well-formed: %s" % ("PASS" if ids_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Cited titles non-empty: %s"
              % ("PASS" if titles_ok else "FAIL"))
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: %s" % run1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: %s" % run2)
        print("Deterministic: %s" % ("MATCH" if run1 == run2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    ThesisProgram().run()
